Page 97 of 108 
 
VIII. Capital Market Conditions 
1 
Q. 
What factors are affecting the cost of equity for regulated utilities in 
2 
the current and projected capital markets? 
3 
A. 
The cost of equity for regulated utility companies is being affected by 
4 
several factors in the current and projected capital markets.  These factors 
5 
include: (1) the market’s expectation for substantially higher interest rates; 
6 
(2) current low yields on utility stocks; and (3) current high valuations on 
7 
utility shares relative to historical levels and relative to the broader market.  
8 
In this section of my Direct Testimony, I will discuss each of these factors 
9 
and how it affects the cost of equity for regulated utilities.  
10 
 
11 
Q. 
What effect do rising interest rates have on the cost of equity?  
12 
A. 
The expectation for increasing interest rates indicates that the calculated 
13 
cost of equity for the proxy companies using current market data is likely 
14 
to be a conservative estimate of investors’ required return.  Consequently, 
15 
rising interest rates support the use of forward-looking assumptions in the 
16 
ROE estimation models and would support selection of a return toward the 
17 
upper end of a reasonable range of cost of equity estimates that are based 
18 
on current market data.   
19 
99


--- Page 102 ---
 
 
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
 
Page 98 of 108 
 
Q. 
What is the financial market’s expectation for increases in short-term 
1 
interest rates by the Federal Reserve?  
2 
A. 
In mid-December 2015 the Federal Reserve announced the first increase in 
3 
interest rates since the financial market collapse in 2008.  In its statement, 
4 
the Federal Reserve indicated that further increases would be gradual as 
5 
the economy strengthens further and inflation rises from undesirably low 
6 
levels. The January 2016 issue of the Blue Chip Financial Forecasts 
7 
(“Blue Chip”) surveyed leading economists and market participants 
8 
concerning their views about the likelihood of future increases in short-
9 
term interest rates by the Federal Reserve.  Blue Chip reports that 73 
10 
percent of market participants surveyed expect the Federal Reserve to 
11 
raise short-term interest rates again at the Federal Open Market Committee 
12 
meeting in March with the expectation that there could be up to three 
13 
short-term interest rate increases in 2016.86   The mid-point of the current 
14 
federal funds rate is 0.125%.   
15 
 
16 
Q. 
What are your conclusions regarding the effect of higher interest rates 
17 
on the cost of equity for gas utilities such as KEDNY and KEDLI? 
18 
A. 
The Federal Reserve’s recent rate increase and the expectation that rates 
19 
will gradually increase over some period of time indicates that the 
20 
                                                 
86  
Blue Chip Financial Forecasts, Volume 35, No. 1, January 1, 2016, at 14. 
100


--- Page 103 ---
 
 
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
 
Page 99 of 108 
 
calculated cost of equity for the proxy companies using any cost of equity 
1 
estimation technique relying on discounted cash flows is likely to lag 
2 
investors’ required return during the period that the Companies’ rates will 
3 
be in effect.  During periods in which interest rates are expected to 
4 
increase, the dividend yields of utility stocks become less attractive for 
5 
income-oriented investors relative to bond yields.  Because many income-
6 
oriented investors hold utility stocks for their dividend yields, such periods 
7 
place pressure on utility share prices relative to the broader market, as 
8 
measured by the S&P 500 Index.  Consequently, a consensus expectation 
9 
of rising interest rates supports selection of a return for the Companies 
10 
based not only on the DCF models, which do not consider interest rates, 
11 
but also a forward-looking CAPM analysis, which does consider interest 
12 
rates.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
How has the period of abnormally low interest rates affected the 
15 
valuation and dividend yields of utility shares? 
16 
A. 
The ROE that is established in these proceedings is intended to reflect 
17 
investors’ required return over the forward-looking period during which 
18 
the established rates will be in effect. The Federal Reserve’s Quantitative 
19 
101


--- Page 104 ---
 
 
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
 
Page 100 of 108 
 
Easing87 program resulted in higher asset prices for many common stocks 
1 
and lower interest rates on government bonds.  The prices of shares of 
2 
public utility companies experienced the higher asset valuations over the 
3 
past several years, as investors sought higher returns and more attractive 
4 
yields than were being offered by bonds.  Consequently, the current share 
5 
price of many utility stocks has increased to levels above Value Line’s 
6 
target price for 2018-2020, while the dividend yield of those same utility 
7 
stocks has declined to unusually low levels.  While federal market 
8 
intervention reduced interest rates on government bonds over the last 
9 
several years, the expectation is that interest rates will be rising over the 
10 
period when the rates that are established for the Companies in these 
11 
proceedings will be in effect.  Therefore, it is not appropriate to base the 
12 
allowed ROE entirely on the historical level of interest rates.  This is 
13 
especially true when there is an expectation that interest rates are at an 
14 
inflection point, as is the current expectation. 
15 
                                                 
87  
The Federal Reserve instituted a policy of Quantitative Easing, which is an 
expansionary monetary policy wherein the Federal Reserve added liquidity to 
capital markets by keeping Treasury yields low.  Because Treasury yields are the 
basis for long-term interest rates, the result is a lower interest rate on other debt 
including corporate bonds. The intention of this program was to lower interest 
rates to stimulate economic growth. The Federal Reserve implemented three 
rounds of Quantitative Easing, which began in December 2008 and concluded in 
October 2014.   
102


--- Page 105 ---
 
 
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
 
Page 101 of 108 
 
Q. 
What conclusions do you draw from your analysis of capital market 
1 
conditions? 
2 
A. 
My primary conclusion is that it is important to consider the effect of 
3 
capital market conditions on the inputs and assumptions used in the ROE 
4 
estimation models and to consider whether those market conditions are 
5 
sustainable over the period that the ROE would be in effect.  In particular, 
6 
it is important to consider the effect of the recent market conditions and 
7 
the expectation of rising interest rates on the result of the ROE estimation 
8 
models. 
9 
 
10 
IX.       Multi-Year Rate Plan 
11 
Q. 
Would a multi-year rate plan impact your ROE recommendation?   
12 
A.  
Yes, it would.  As noted earlier in my Direct Testimony, Treasury yields 
13 
are at or near all-time lows largely as the result of the combined effects of 
14 
the Federal Reserve’s monetary policy and a relatively slow economic 
15 
recovery.  Analysts’ consensus forecasts expect that interest rates will be 
16 
increasing in the short term.  While the current three month average yield 
17 
on the 30-year U.S. Treasury bond as of October 30, 2015 was 2.90 
18 
percent, the latest Blue Chip Consensus Forecast for the period 2017 to 
19 
2021 is 4.80 percent, an increase of approximately 190 basis points.  If 
20 
103


--- Page 106 ---
 
 
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
 
Page 102 of 108 
 
interest rates were to increase as predicted, an ROE established based on 
1 
economic conditions in 2015 will not reflect economic conditions during 
2 
the term of a multi-year rate plan.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
How might the risks of a multi-year rate plan be addressed? 
5 
A. 
The Commission has in many cases approved three-year rate case 
6 
settlements that often include stay-out premiums.  It is my understanding 
7 
that the Companies will provide three years of forecast data in their rate 
8 
filings.  In keeping with Commission precedent, a stay-out premium 
9 
would reflect the increased risk faced by the Companies under a multi-
10 
year rate plan. 
11 
 
12 
Q.  
How has New York typically estimated a stay-out premium?  
13 
A.  
The New York approach has typically set the measure of the risk and 
14 
return trade-off using one half of the yield spread between a one-year and 
15 
three-year Treasury securities.   
16 
 
17 
Q. 
How can the implications of a multi-year rate plan be better reflected 
18 
in a stay-out premium?  
19 
A. 
In addition to the yield spread between the one-year and three-year 
20 
Treasury securities, it would be important to consider the yield spread 
21 
104


--- Page 107 ---
 
 
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
 
Page 103 of 108 
 
between corporate bonds of the same maturities.  Using this methodology, 
1 
the return associated with the stay-out period would include one half of the 
2 
yield spread between the one-year and three-year Treasuries, as typically 
3 
considered by the Commission, and the difference in the yield spread 
4 
between one-year and three-year Treasuries and one-year and three-year 
5 
corporate bonds.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Have you calculated the difference in the expected yield on one-year 
8 
and three-year Treasuries? 
9 
A.  
Yes.  As shown in Exhibit __ (AEB-15), the six month average yield 
10 
spread between one and three-year Treasury bonds was 70 basis points.  
11 
As shown on Chart 6 and Chart 7, the yield spread between the one-year 
12 
and three-year Treasuries expanded significantly beginning in the Great 
13 
Recession and has remained very wide as compared with pre-recessionary 
14 
periods.   
15 
105

rated corporate bonds is approximately 15 basis points.  
1 
 
2 
Q. 
What are the implications of a multi-year rate plan on the ROE?  
3 
A. 
The stay out premium associated with a multi-year rate plan should not 
4 
only compensate utility investors for changes in the level of interest rates 
5 
or inflation, which is all that a stay-out premium based on comparing one-
6 
year and three-year Treasury rates does, but also compensate utility 
7 
investors for the potential risk of under-earning that is introduced by 
8 
“staying out.”  By staying out of rate cases, the utility may not fully 
9 
recover material amounts of capital expenditures and may be required to 
10 
absorb losses due to differences between the cost of service established in 
11 
the rate plan and actual levels of revenue and expense.  The premium 
12 
should compensate the utility for these additional risks over and above 
13 
interest rate risk.  The stay out premium should be sufficient to 
14 
compensate the utility for interest rate risk and for the heightened business 
15 
risk from increased financial exposure the utility faces by staying out.  In 
16 
the current market environment there is additional risk that the authorized 
17 
ROE will be lower than investors’ expectations as interest rates are 
18 
expected to increase.   
19 
108


--- Page 111 ---
 
 
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
 
Page 107 of 108 
 
Q. 
What do you propose as the stay-out premium for a three-year rate 
1 
plan?  
2 
A. 
The ROE proposed by the Company of 9.94 percent will not provide the 
3 
Companies a return commensurate with the return available on 
4 
investments of similar risk over the term of the multi-year rate plan 
5 
without an adequate stay-out premium.  Based on the analysis discussed 
6 
above, the stay-out premium would be estimated in two components: 1) 50 
7 
percent of the yield spread on the Treasury bonds (one-half of the 70 basis 
8 
point spread or 35 basis points) over the stay-out period, reflecting the 
9 
effect of the change in interest rates on ROE; and 2) the difference in yield 
10 
spreads on Treasury bonds and A-rated corporate bonds of 15 basis points, 
11 
resulting in a stay-out premium of approximately 50 basis points.  
12 
 
13 
X.       Conclusion and Recommendation 
14 
Q. 
What is your conclusion regarding a fair return on book equity for 
15 
KEDNY and KEDLI? 
16 
A. 
My recommended return on book equity considers the results of the DCF 
17 
and CAPM models, summarized in Table 8 (below), and the specific risks 
18 
to which the Companies are exposed.  The range established based on an 
19 
equal weighting of the DCF and CAPM model results is between 9.89 
20 
109


--- Page 112 ---
 
 
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
 
Page 108 of 108 
 
percent and 10.42 percent.  The Companies’ requested ROE of 9.94 
1 
percent is conservative and should be adopted.  Furthermore, if the 
2 
Commission approves the stay-out period, a premium of 50 basis points 
3 
should be added to the ROE.  
4

ANN E. BULKLEY 
VICE PRESIDENT 
Ms. Bulkley has nearly two decades of management and economic consulting experience in 
1 
the energy industry.  Ms. Bulkley has extensive state and federal regulatory experience on 
2 
both electric and natural gas issues including rate of return, cost of equity and capital structure 
3 
issues. Ms. Bulkley has advised clients seeking to acquire utility assets, providing valuation 
4 
services including an understanding of regulation, market expected returns, and the 
5 
assessment of utility risk factors.   Ms. Bulkley has assisted clients with valuations of public 
6 
utility and industrial properties for ratemaking, purchase and sale considerations, ad valorem 
7 
tax assessments, and accounting and financial purposes.  In addition, Ms. Bulkley has 
8

9 
restructuring and regulatory and litigation support.

I. 
Introduction and Qualifications 
1 
Q. 
Please state your name and business address. 
2 
A. 
My name is Keri Sweet Zavaglia.  My business address is 300 Erie 
3 
Boulevard West, Syracuse, New York 13202.  
4 
 
5 
Q. 
By whom are you employed and in what capacity? 
6 
A. 
I am employed by National Grid USA Service Company, Inc. (“Service 
7 
Company”) as Vice President, New York Performance & Strategy.  
8 
Service Company provides corporate support and other services to all of 
9 
National Grid plc’s US affiliates, including The Brooklyn Union Gas 
10 
Company d/b/a National Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East 
11 
Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”).  I am responsible for the 
12 
performance management of the New York businesses (KEDNY, KEDLI 
13 
and Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
14 
(collectively, “National Grid” or the “Company”)) and executing their 
15 
business strategy.   
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Please describe your educational background and business 
18 
experience.     
19 
A. 
I received a Bachelor of Arts degree in Journalism, Public Relations and 
20 
Advertising from Temple University in 1999.  I received a Juris Doctorate 
21 
190


--- Page 212 ---
Testimony of Keri Sweet Zavaglia 
Page 2 of 15 
 
from Temple University Beasley School of Law in 2002 and served as an 
1 
Assistant District Attorney in Philadelphia, Pennsylvania for more than 
2 
three years.  I have worked for National Grid for nearly ten years, 
3 
primarily as an attorney for the Company in various roles in the New York 
4 
Regulatory Legal group.  For approximately one year I have served as the 
5 
Acting Vice President of Gas Operations, Maintenance and Construction, 
6 
for Upstate New York and assumed my current role at the beginning of 
7 
this year.   
8 
 
9 
II. 
Purpose of the Testimony 
10 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your testimony? 
11 
A. 
The purpose of my testimony is to describe National Grid’s progress in 
12 
implementing the recommendations from the recently concluded 
13 
Comprehensive Management and Operations Audit (“Management 
14 
Audit”) of National Grid’s New York gas business (Case 13-G-0009), 
15 
conducted by NorthStar Consulting Group (“NorthStar”).     
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Are you sponsoring any exhibits through your testimony? 
18 
A. 
Yes.  I sponsor two exhibits that were prepared under my direction and 
19 
supervision:   
20

8 
 
9 
III. 
Management Audit Implementation Plan 
10 
Q. 
Please provide a brief summary of the Management Audit process. 
11 
A. 
In February 2013, the Commission authorized issuance of a Request for 
12 
Proposals (“RFP”) for an independent third-party consultant to conduct an 
13 
audit of National Grid’s New York gas business in accordance with 
14 
Section 66(19) of the Public Service Law (“PSL”).  In June 2013, the 
15 
Commission selected NorthStar to conduct the audit under the supervision 
16 
of Department of Public Service Staff (“Staff”).  From August 2013 
17 
through July 2014, NorthStar audited National Grid’s New York gas 
18 
business, focusing on eight traditional audit areas:   
19 
 Corporate mission, objectives, goals and planning  
20 
 Load forecasting  
21 
192


--- Page 214 ---
Testimony of Keri Sweet Zavaglia 
Page 4 of 15 
 
 Supply procurement  
1 
 System planning  
2 
 Capital and operations and maintenance (“O&M”) budgeting  
3 
 Program and project planning and management  
4 
 Work force management   
5 
 Performance and results measurement 
6 
 
7 
In addition, the Commission directed that the scope of the Management 
8 
Audit include an assessment of National Grid’s information systems, 
9 
including the “adequacy and efficiency of the customer information and 
10 
billing systems used to support management information and customer 
11 
service operations.” 
12 
 
13 
NorthStar, in coordination with Staff, conducted a thorough and 
14 
professional audit marked by open and productive discussions among the 
15 
parties.  Throughout the Management Audit, National Grid fully 
16 
cooperated with NorthStar in the interest of supporting audit 
17 
recommendations that were well-informed and meaningful.  NorthStar 
18 
received full access to National Grid’s personnel, records and systems 
19 
over a ten-month discovery period during which National Grid answered 
20 
193


--- Page 215 ---
Testimony of Keri Sweet Zavaglia 
Page 5 of 15 
 
more than 700 data requests and participated in over 200 management 
1 
interviews.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe the results of the Management Audit. 
4 
A. 
In August 2014, NorthStar issued its audit report (“Audit Report”) that 
5 
described generally strong performance by National Grid in a number of 
6 
key functional areas, including gas operations, load forecasting, supply 
7 
procurement and customer information systems.  The Audit Report offered 
8 
31 recommendations aimed at improving National Grid’s performance.  
9 
The Commission released the Audit Report on October 2, 2014. 
10 
 
11 
While the Company did not agree with all of NorthStar’s findings and 
12 
conclusions, overall, NorthStar’s recommendations provide a constructive 
13 
means of enhancing the day-to-day performance of the New York gas 
14 
business.  Accordingly, National Grid generally accepted the 
15 
recommendations presented in the Audit Report.   
16 
 
17 
 
On November 3, 2014, the Company filed an initial Implementation Plan 
18 
with the Commission detailing its plans to implement the Management 
19 
Audit recommendations.  Thereafter, National Grid and Staff collaborated 
20 
to review and discuss the initial Implementation Plan.  Staff determined 
21 
194


--- Page 216 ---
Testimony of Keri Sweet Zavaglia 
Page 6 of 15 
 
that the initial Implementation Plan was generally acceptable, but 
1 
recommended certain changes and clarifications.  Based on feedback from 
2 
Staff, National Grid filed a revised Implementation Plan with the 
3 
Commission on April 21, 2015. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Did the Commission accept National Grid’s Implementation Plan? 
6 
A. 
Yes.  On May 14, 2015, the Commission issued an “Order Approving an 
7 
Implementation Plan” (“Order”).  In the Order, the Commission approved 
8 
National Grid’s revised Implementation Plan and directed the Company to 
9 
implement the proposed actions described therein.     
10 
 
11 
Q.  
Please describe National Grid’s approach to implementing the 
12 
Management Audit recommendations. 
13 
A.  
National Grid viewed the Management Audit process as an opportunity to 
14 
assess and improve its management of gas operations, systems, 
15 
governance and processes as part of its ongoing effort to drive overall 
16 
improvement for the benefit of customers.  National Grid has appreciated 
17 
the collaborative efforts of NorthStar and Staff throughout the process and 
18 
recognizes that the Management Audit recommendations provide positive 
19 
and constructive insights about how the Company may improve its 
20 
processes.  Therefore, the Company is making every effort to implement 
21 
195


--- Page 217 ---
Testimony of Keri Sweet Zavaglia 
Page 7 of 15 
 
the Management Audit recommendations in a manner that best achieves 
1 
their intent. 
2 
 
3 
The Implementation Plan reflects the Company’s consideration of the 
4 
Audit Report recommendations and desire to realize the benefits identified 
5 
by NorthStar, as well as the collaboration with Staff that helped to 
6 
improve it.  Accordingly, the Implementation Plan outlines constructive, 
7 
cost effective solutions to the Management Audit recommendations that 
8 
will enhance National Grid’s ability to provide service to our customers in 
9 
New York.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
What does the Order require with regard to reporting on the status of 
12 
the Implementation Plan? 
13 
A. 
National Grid must file a written update to the Implementation Plan at 
14 
least every four months.  The update must describe the implementation 
15 
status of each recommendation, implementation action steps (major 
16 
activities), and associated implementation schedules.  The Company meets 
17 
with Staff midway between each update to provide additional updates and 
18 
discuss some items in further detail.  These reporting requirements 
19 
continue until Staff determines that the Implementation Plan is fully 
20 
implemented.  In addition to the formal reporting requirements, the 
21 
196


--- Page 218 ---
Testimony of Keri Sweet Zavaglia 
Page 8 of 15 
 
Company and Staff have periodically met to address key initiatives the 
1 
Company is undertaking, such as the recommendations regarding National 
2 
Grid’s gas supply procurement and forecasting functions.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
What is the current status of the Company’s compliance with the 
5 
Implementation Plan? 
6 
A. 
As of September 14, 2015, when the Company submitted its first 
7 
Implementation Plan Update in accordance with the Order, the Company 
8 
identified 13 recommendations as “Pending Review” and 18 
9 
recommendations as “In Progress.”  “Pending Review” means the 
10 
Company’s actions to implement the recommendation have been 
11 
completed and are pending review by Staff.  Upon acceptance by Staff, the 
12 
recommendation is deemed “implemented.”  A recommendation that is “In 
13 
Progress” has implementation action steps that are either ongoing or on 
14 
hold pending other decisions or activities.  A summary of the current 
15 
status is attached as Exhibit __ (KSZ-2). 
16 
 
17 
 
The following are highlights from the Company’s initial Implementation 
18 
Plan Update:   
19 
 Board Governance - NorthStar found that the Board of Directors of 
20 
National Grid USA would benefit from additional independence 
21 
197


--- Page 219 ---
Testimony of Keri Sweet Zavaglia 
Page 9 of 15 
 
from US management.  Accordingly, in May 2015, the Company 
1 
appointed an independent Director (Thérèse Esperdy) to serve on 
2 
the Board of Directors of National Grid USA.  For the New York 
3 
operating companies’ boards, NorthStar similarly found there was 
4 
too much overlap with the board members and the New York 
5 
Leadership team.  However, Staff and the Company subsequently 
6 
agreed that the New York operating companies would benefit from 
7 
boards members who are familiar with the unique operating, 
8 
financial and regulatory challenges facing the New York 
9 
businesses.  Accordingly, National Grid appointed a majority of 
10 
directors who are members of the New York jurisdictional 
11 
management team.  In addition, the National Grid USA and New 
12 
York operating company boards adopted terms of reference that 
13 
clearly define these boards’ roles and responsibilities. 
14 
 
15 
 Jurisdictional Operating Model - While NorthStar found the 
16 
current model to be a significant improvement over prior 
17 
management structures, the Audit Report recommends, inter alia, 
18 
that the Company “continue to evolve” the jurisdictional operating 
19 
model to provide a clear command structure for the New York 
20 
Jurisdictional President.  Accordingly, in April 2015, the Company 
21 
198


--- Page 220 ---
Testimony of Keri Sweet Zavaglia 
Page 10 of 15 
 
modified its jurisdictional model to enhance operational 
1 
accountability to the New York Jurisdictional President.  Key 
2 
members of the New York Management Team now report directly 
3 
to the New York Jurisdictional President.  In addition, effective 
4 
April 1, 2015, employees reporting to the Vice Presidents of New 
5 
York Electric and Gas Operations moved to the New York 
6 
Jurisdiction.  As a result, more than 2,600 electric and gas 
7 
operations personnel supporting the New York utilities now report 
8 
under the New York Jurisdictional President. 
9 
 
10 
 U.S. Chief Risk and Compliance Officer - Consistent with 
11 
NorthStar’s recommendation, National Grid’s US General Counsel 
12 
was appointed the US Chief Risk and Compliance Officer.  The 
13 
US Chief Risk and Compliance Officer is responsible for 
14 
coordinating, reviewing and challenging reports to US 
15 
management and the National Grid USA Board by the Company’s 
16 
Risk, Compliance and Ethics groups.  National Grid also appointed 
17 
a new Global Head of Assurance who is primarily responsible for 
18 
coordinating, reviewing and challenging the assessment findings of 
19 
various US risk assessment groups.   
20 
 
21 
199

 Estimating Center of Excellence - National Grid is developing a 
1

2 
organization.  This new group will support development of a 
3 
consistent estimating end-to-end process, including documentation 
4 
of responsibilities and key performance indicators to measure 
5 
performance.   
6 
 
7 
The Company’s Implementation Plan Update also described progress on 
8 
the various recommendations intended to improve performance in the 
9 
project management, work management, system planning, gas forecasting 
10 
and gas supply procurement functional areas.  The Company has also 
11 
delivered reports on two financial issues impacting KEDNY and KEDLI: 
12 
(i) an updated analysis of the stranded, incremental and re-allocated costs 
13 
resulting from the loss of the Long Island Power Authority management 
14

15

16 
Foundation Program and Finance Remediation Plan (submitted to Staff in 
17 
April 2015).  These reports are discussed in more detail in the testimony of 
18 
the Revenue Requirements and Shared Services panels.

 Q.  
Please describe further the category of recommendations that are “In 
1 
  
Progress.” 
2 
A.  
The Company is mindful of the need to implement the recommendations 
3 
in the most efficient and effective manner possible.  Many of the “In 
4 
Progress” recommendations remain open pending longer-term initiatives 
5 
or external input.  For example, implementation of several 
6 
recommendations depends on new applications and systems, such as the 
7 
recommendation to implement a program to track and manage crew and 
8 
individual worker productivity (Recommendation VII-1).  The Company 
9 
continues to work to complete all open recommendations on the schedule 
10 
identified in the Implementation Plan.   
11 
 
12 
IV. 
Management Audit Implementation Costs 
13 
Q. 
Did the Management Audit consider the costs to implement and any 
14 
savings associated with the recommendations?  
15 
A. 
Yes, in the RFP for the Management Audit, the Commission directed that 
16 
the “recommendations developed by the [auditor] should be accompanied 
17 
by customer benefit analyses which take into account, among other things, 
18 
one-time and ongoing costs, potential benefits and risks, and potential 
19 
savings or efficiencies.”  Accordingly, NorthStar filed a separate 
20 
“customer benefit analysis” (“CBA”) that describes the expected costs and 
21 
201


--- Page 223 ---
Testimony of Keri Sweet Zavaglia 
Page 13 of 15 
 
benefits resulting from the implementation of each recommendation and, 
1 
where applicable, a five-year payback analysis.  NorthStar’s estimates of 
2 
implementation costs and any potential savings for each of the 
3 
recommendations are summarized in Exhibit __ (KSZ-1).  
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Are the costs to implement the recommendations reflected in KEDNY 
6 
and KEDLI’s revenue requirements?  
7 
A. 
Several of the recommendations relate to activities the Company was 
8 
already undertaking consistent with its commitment to continuous 
9 
improvement (e.g., developing an integrated natural gas system-wide plan 
10 
(Recommendation V-1)).  They are reflected in KEDNY and KEDLI’s 
11 
revenue requirements.  On the other hand, there are a number of other 
12 
recommendations that give rise to new, incremental initiatives.  The 
13 
Company’s Implementation Plan includes preliminary estimates of the 
14 
incremental costs of these new initiatives needed to implement the 
15 
recommendations.    
16 
 
17 
To the extent KEDNY and KEDLI incurred costs in the 12-month period 
18 
ended September 30, 2015 (“Historic Test Year”) to implement the 
19 
Management Audit recommendations, those costs were de minimis.  
20 
KEDNY and KEDLI are, however, proposing to recover recurring 
21 
202


--- Page 224 ---
Testimony of Keri Sweet Zavaglia 
Page 14 of 15 
 
implementation costs in the Rate Year (e.g., the ongoing labor and systems 
1 
costs to develop a Gas Estimating Department (Recommendation VI-1)).  
2 
Those costs are also reflected in the revenue requirements.    
3 
 
4 
Q.  
What types of benefits from the recommendations are identified in the 
5 
CBA? 
6 
A.  
NorthStar identified several primary categories of benefits of 
7 
implementing the recommendations, including:   
8 
 Enhancing corporate governance and decision making 
9 
 Improving work management and tracking 
10 
 Increasing data availability and analysis 
11 
 Improving work methods and processes 
12 
Potential Company and customer benefits of implementing the various 
13 
recommendations include reliability and safety improvements, customer 
14 
service enhancements, future cost avoidance, and risk or volatility 
15 
reduction.  The benefits may be one-time, enduring or of limited duration.  
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Did NorthStar identify cost savings for all of the Management Audit 
18 
recommendations? If so, are they reflected in the Rate Year? 
19 
A. 
While NorthStar’s CBA identified qualitative benefits for all of the 
20 
recommendations, most recommendations do not have quantifiable cost 
21 
203


--- Page 225 ---
Testimony of Keri Sweet Zavaglia 
Page 15 of 15 
 
savings.  For example, Recommendation III-1, directed at recruiting and 
1 
appointing an independent Director for the NG USA Board, will benefit 
2 
the Company and customers, but such benefits are not readily measured.   
3 
 
4 
For the recommendations where NorthStar identified potential cost 
5 
savings, those savings are not separately reflected in the Rate Year 
6 
because (i) the recommendation involves costs that are not included in 
7 
KEDNY or KEDLI’s cost of service because they are pass through items 
8 
(e.g., the recommendations relating to gas supply procurement 
9 
(Recommendation VIII-2)) or (ii) the recommendation is not yet 
10 
implemented and the cost to implement is expected to outweigh any 
11 
savings over the next few years (e.g., work productivity tracking systems 
12 
(Recommendation VII-1)).  
13 
 
14 
Q.   
Does that conclude your testimony? 
15 
A. 
Yes it does. 
16

INTRODUCTION 
 
A. Background 
 
In accordance with the Commission’s May 14, 2015 Order1, The Brooklyn Union Gas Company 
d/b/a National Grid NY, KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid and Niagara Mohawk 
Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid (collectively, “National Grid” or the “Company”) offer the 
initial Implementation Plan Update in connection with the August 2014 “Comprehensive 
Management and Operations Audit of National Grid USA’s New York Gas Companies” (the 
“Audit Report”) prepared by NorthStar Consulting Group.  The Audit Report contains thirty-one 
recommendations aimed at improving the Company’s performance.  For each recommendation, 
the Company’s Implementation Plan (filed on April 21, 2015) sets forth: the Company’s proposal 
to implement the recommendation; the team leads and executive sponsor; an implementation 
schedule; a cost/benefit analysis associated with implementing the recommendation; and an 
implementation priority.   This Implementation Plan Update describes the Company’s progress 
implementing each recommendation. 
 
B. Implementation Plan Progress Summary 
 
Table 1 provides a snapshot of implementation progress on the thirty-one recommendations in the 
Audit Report: 
 
                     Table 1—Implementation Progress—Aggregate 
 
Total Recommendations 
Implemented 
0 
Pending Review 
13 
In Progress 
18 
 
The three implementation status categories in Table 1 are defined as follows: 
 
• Implemented – Action steps associated with implementing a recommendation have been 
completed, reviewed and accepted by Staff. 
 
• Pending Review - Action steps associated with implementing a recommendation have 
been completed, and are pending review by Staff. Acceptance of a recommendation as 
complete will be determined following Staff’s review. 
 
• In Progress – Actions associated with implementing a recommendation are on-going or on 
hold pending other decisions or activities.

IMPLEMENTATION PLAN UPDATES 
 
Chapter III: Corporate Mission, Objectives, Goals and Planning  
Recommendation III-1 and 2 
Recommendation III-1.  Reconstitute the NG USA BOD by: 1) limiting the number of 
members who are also part of the US Executive Team, NG USA Officers and Senior Managers to 
no more than two; 2) appointing at least one qualified truly independent Director who is not 
employed by any National Grid company; and 3) filling the remainder of Director seats with 
either NG UK or NG-plc executives, or other independent, qualified individuals.  Define the 
roles and responsibilities of the NG USA BOD to include those typical of a corporate BOD, 
including review of financial performance and external auditor reports, review of risks, approval 
of both capital and operating budgets, and the ability and expectation to challenge and reject 
recommended projects and actions. 
Recommendation III-2.  Reconstitute the BODs for KEDLI, KEDNY, and NMPC (“New York 
Operating Companies”) by: 1) limiting the number of members who are part of the New York 
Jurisdictional team, regardless of reporting relationship, to no more than one Director; and 2) 
filling the remaining two Director positions with executives from the other US jurisdictions or 
NG USA who do not have responsibilities for New York operations.  Define the roles and 
responsibilities of the subsidiary boards to include review of operating and financial 
performance, review of relevant external auditor statements, approval of service levels and 
budgets (e.g., SLAs), and approval of specific plans impacting their service territory, such as rate 
filings, major capital projects, and significant customer programs. 
 
Implementation Plan Leads 
Executive Sponsor: 
Ronald Macklin, US General Counsel 
Ken Daly, President – New York Jurisdiction 
Team Lead(s): 
Timothy McAllister, Assistant General Counsel and Director 
Philip DeCicco, Assistant General Counsel and Director 
 
Implementation Priority: III-1 High; III-2 Medium   
 
 
 
Recommendation Accepted: III-1 Yes (with modifications); III-2 Yes (with modifications) 
 
 
Current Status: In Progress (III-1)/Pending Review (III-2) 
 
NG USA Board of Directors 
 
In the revised New York Gas Management Audit Implementation Plan, the Company proposed to 
reconstitute NG USA’s Board in a manner generally consistent with the recommendation: 
• No more than three (3) directors will be members of the US management team; 
• One director will be an independent director who is not an employee of the Company; and 
• Other directors will be either NG UK or NG-plc executives, or other independent, qualified 
Exhibit __ (KSZ-2) 
Page 5 of 45
218


--- Page 243 ---
6 
 
individuals. 
 
In May 2015, the Company appointed a three person board, consisting of two US senior 
management members, and one independent director (Thérèse Esperdy).  Ms. Esperdy has served 
as a Non-executive Director of the Board of National Grid plc since March 2014 and currently 
chairs the Finance Committee.  Because of potential tax implications, at that time, the Company 
elected not to appoint additional NG UK directors. The Company has since appointed a third 
director (Cheri Warren) who is member of the US management team.       
 
In addition, the NG USA Board (and each of the New York Operating Companies’ boards) has 
adopted terms of reference that clearly define the board’s roles and responsibilities.

New York Operating Companies’ Board of Directors 
For the New York Operating Companies’ boards, following the release of the Audit Report, 
National Grid and Staff discussed maintaining a New York focus on these boards by appointing a 
majority of New York directors (notwithstanding the Audit Report recommendation to appoint 
non-New York managers).  The Company believes it is important that the operating companies’ 
directors are familiar with the unique operating, financial and regulatory challenges facing the 
New York businesses.  Accordingly, as described in the revised New York Gas Management 
Audit Implementation Plan, the Company appointed a majority of directors on the New York 
operating company boards who are members of the New York Jurisdictional management team.

              Chapter III – Corporate Mission, Objectives, Goals and Planning           
Recommendation III-3 
Continue to evolve the Jurisdictional organization model to establish a clear command and control 
structure for the NY Jurisdictional President, as described by NG USA executives during the audit.  
• Improvements should include direct organizational reporting relationships between the NY 
Jurisdictional President and the full-time dedicated, senior managers who are responsible 
for the core utility operations and performance of the NY utilities.  
• The NY Jurisdictional President should have direct reporting and control over 
NY-dedicated support personnel that plan, execute, monitor and control activities in 
support of the NY jurisdiction and operating companies.  
• As indicated by NG USA Executives, this evolution is not a major reorganization nor 
should it result in increased headcount, but rather a re-alignment of reporting 
responsibilities and clarification of roles and responsibilities.  
 
Implementation Plan Leads  
Executive Sponsor: 
Dean Seavers, President – NG USA 
Ken Daly, President – New York Jurisdiction 
Team Lead(s): 
Roger Young, SVP – US Human Resources 
 
 
Implementation Priority: High      
 
 
Recommendation Accepted: Yes 
 
Current Status: Pending Review  
 
NY Jurisdiction Organization Design  
 
In April 2015, the Company modified its jurisdictional model to enhance operational 
accountability to the NY Jurisdictional President.  Specifically, as described in the revised New 
York Gas Management Audit Implementation Plan, key members of the NY Leadership Team 
now report directly to the New York Jurisdictional President.  These direct reports, which 
currently include senior managers from Gas Operations, Electric Operations and Network 
Strategy, are accountable to the NY Jurisdictional President and their performance will be 
evaluated based on the success of the New York Operating Companies.  
 
In addition to the realignment of key members of the New York Management Team, effective 
April 1, 2015, employees reporting to the Vice Presidents of New York Electric and Gas 
Operations moved to the NY Jurisdiction.  As a result, more than 2,600 electric and gas 
operations personnel supporting the New York utilities now report under the NY Jurisdictional 
Exhibit __ (KSZ-2) 
Page 7 of 45
220


--- Page 245 ---
8 
 
President. 
 
With regard to the New York regulatory function, the Company has reconsidered and determined 
that the NY Regulatory and Pricing officer should report directly to the Senior Vice President for 
US Regulation, and report to the NY Jurisdictional President in a matrix reporting relationship.  
This approach allows National Grid to better coordinate its regulatory strategy across the US 
business, while at the same time providing for a senior, dedicated New York regulatory officer to 
coordinate with the NY Jurisdictional President on significant regulatory matters. 
 
Organization design work to strengthen the jurisdictional model is ongoing.  Going forward, the 
Company will continue to review the jurisdictional model to identify if additional adjustments are 
appropriate and beneficial. 
 
Cross-Jurisdictional Coordinating Review 
 
To further allow the Jurisdictional Presidents to monitor and review functional performance by the 
service company, National Grid established a cross-jurisdictional coordinating review (“CJR”) 
process, with executive representation from the jurisdictions and US functions. The CJR will take 
place monthly to undertake an operating company by operating company performance review, 
including a review of performance against SLA metrics.

Recommendation III-4     
Establish a Chief Risk Officer within the NG USA organization, reporting to the President 
NGUSA, with responsibility and appropriate authority, for coordinating, reviewing and 
challenging the results of all the various risk assessment groups, including the CRT, the 
CET/SOX compliance, Ethics and Compliance, and IAD to identify risk trends, track and 
manage financial and operating risks with materiality below NG-plc levels, and monitor that the 
plans prepared by the risk owners are appropriate and represent the best cost solution.  This 
individual needs sufficient authority to direct changes in response to risks, and needs to remain 
sufficiently independent of the operations that they are able to identify patterns, and challenge 
assumptions patterns with impartiality.  This individual should also work to implement 
necessary changes in the NG USA internal controls processes so that, as part of the controls 
processes, sufficient attention is paid to findings that are material at lower reporting levels, and 
gaps/issues are brought to the attention of the relevant Jurisdictional President and the President, 
NG USA.

Ronald Macklin, US General Counsel (for Dean Seavers, 
President – NG USA and Alison Kay, Group General Counsel & 
Company Secretary)

Ronald Macklin, US General Counsel

Recommendation Accepted: Yes (with modifications) 
 
Current Status: Pending Review 
 
US Chief Risk and Compliance Officer 
 
Consistent with NorthStar’s recommendation, National Grid’s US General Counsel was appointed 
the US Chief Risk and Compliance Officer.  The US Chief Risk and Compliance Officer is 
responsible for coordinating, reviewing and challenging the results of the risk, compliance and 
ethics groups, reporting to US management and the NG USA BOD, where appropriate. 
 
In addition, as described in the Implementation Plan, National Grid appointed a new Global Head 
of Assurance (with a reporting relationship to the US Chief Risk and Compliance Officer) who is 
primarily responsible for coordinating, reviewing and challenging the results of various risk 
assessment groups, including compliance and ethics.  The Global Head of Assurance is also 
responsible for defining risks, providing assurance and ensuring consistency across all National

Chapter III – Corporate Mission, Objectives, Goals and Planning   
Recommendation III-6  
Conduct, or contract with qualified outside auditor for, an investigation into the cost impacts of 
the LIPA separation on remaining NY operating company ratepayers, and provide a report to the 
PSC staff within six months.  The investigation should include: 
• True stranded costs broken out by labor and non-labor sources, and by capital 
investments and operating charges 
• Expected revenues by basis (e.g., real estate, IT) and the time period for the revenue 
agreement. 
• Incremental costs, both labor and non-labor, and capital and on-going operating costs or 
benefits 
• Allocated costs (labor and non-labor combined if necessary), and an estimate of the 
aggregate impact of the reallocation of these costs on the remaining NY utilities. 
The investigation should include a broad review of activities and functions that were performed 
with, or for the benefit of LIPA, rather than relying solely on prior listings of impacted areas. 
Where applicable and reasonable, existing studies can be used as sources for the cost estimates, 
with adjustments clearly specified.  The investigation seeks to define and document the costs 
associated with the separation for future use. 
 
 
Implementation Plan Leads  
Executive Sponsor: 
Sharon Partridge, Vice President – Financial Planning & 
Analysis & Decision Support 
David Doxsee, Vice President – Finance (New York) 
Team Lead(s): 
Chip Benson, Director – Finance Business Partners

Recommendation Accepted: Yes 
 
Current Status: Pending Review 
 
As described in the Implementation Plan, the Company updated its analysis of the stranded, 
incremental and re-allocated costs resulting from the loss of the of the LIPA management 
agreement. The Company’s analysis includes an assessment of the following transition cost items: 
Exhibit __ (KSZ-2) 
Page 12 of 45
225

(i) an accounting of stranded costs (capital and O&M) resulting from the LIPA transition broken

and (iv) offsetting LIPA transition charges paid by PSEG-LI. 
The Company provided its LIPA transition cost analysis to Staff in September 2015.

Chapter IV – Capital and O&M Budgeting  
 
 
       
Recommendation IV-1  
Prepare a report for submittal to the PSC staff within six months that fully documents the capital 
and O&M costs associated with USFP, USFP Stabilization, Finance Remediation and other 
financial and IS system related initiatives so that ratepayers are protected from SAP-related costs 
in excess of levels agreed upon in the previous NMPC and KEDNY rate cases (and for KEDLI 
by default).  At a minimum, NG USA should: 
• Document and re-establish the original capital and O&M costs underlying the rate case 
level. 
• Clearly and specifically define the distinctions (e.g., timing, scope and cost) between the 
original USFP project, the USFP Stabilization Project, other SAP/USFP-related projects, 
the Finance Remediation Project and other financial and IS system activities. 
• Specify how the costs associated with each of these activities are being tracked, 
monitored and verified, including: the specific project or cost codes used for dedicated 
internal labor, part-time or as-needed internal labor, contractors, other direct, indirect 
and capital costs, and the procedures for review, verification, challenge and correction of 
costs.  
• Explain and document the impact of back-filling positions assigned to these initiatives. 
• Explain and document the sources of the increase in “other IS” capital and O&M 
expenditures (see Exhibit IV-11) to confirm the costs are not related to the USFP 
Stabilization initiatives. 
 
The purpose of the report is to provide the PSC staff with contemporaneous documentation so 
the costs for fixing USFP/SAP will not be borne by ratepayers, and the costs of Finance 
Remediation and other IS issues can be appropriately assessed in future rate cases.  National 
Grid would continue to have the right to justify costs for SAP enhancements and other initiatives 
for inclusion in rates as a part of the normal rate case process. 
 
 
Implementation Plan Leads 
Executive Sponsor: 
Vivienne Bracken, SVP – Shared Services 
Team Lead(s): 
Rachel Patton – Finance 
 
 
Implementation Priority: High      
 
 
Recommendation Accepted: Yes (with modifications)

Consistent with NorthStar’s recommendation, the Company prepared a report that identified the 
capital and O&M costs associated with USFP, USFP Stabilization, incremental Finance 
Remediation costs and the capital costs associated with other IS system-related initiatives. 
Specifically, the Company’s report (i) identified the original capital and O&M costs associated 
with USFP as currently included in rates; (ii) identified actual O&M and capital costs associated 
with USFP, USFP Stabilization, and incremental Finance Remediation costs; (iii) explained the 
distinctions (i.e., timing, scope and cost) among the original USFP project, the USFP Stabilization 
Project, and the Finance Remediation Project; and (iv) explained how the costs associated with 
each of these activities are being tracked, monitored and verified. 
 
The Company submitted its USFP cost report to Staff in April 2015.

Chapter IV – Capital and O&M Budgeting    
Recommendations IV-2 and XI-2 
• Develop improved SLAs to govern the relationship between the jurisdictional operating 
companies and ServCo.  For products and services provided to NYS utilities from the 
ServCo, SLAs must emulate commercial agreements and should include: 
o 
Improved dialogue among the various National Grid management teams. 
o 
Detailed metrics addressing product and service units, volumes provided, timeframe, 
quality and unit prices. 
o 
Tracking mechanisms including quantifiable and meaningful KPIs. 
o 
Standardized reports across all NG USA entities. 
o 
Enforcement via payment only for product and service units actually provided. 
o 
Jurisdictional management authority to terminate and change service providers. 
• Continue to evolve the SLAs to include additional KPIs addressing each of the major 
functions performed, include measures of efficiency, cost-effectiveness and unit costs, 
provide greater budgetary detail, include financial penalties for failure to achieve 
performance targets, relate the service company employee performance evaluation process 
to the SLAs, require more frequent reporting, incorporate the results of the benchmarking 
exercises, and improve performance targets. 
 
Implementation Priority: Medium     
 
 
 
Recommendation Accepted: Yes (with modifications) 
Implementation Plan Leads  
Executive Sponsor: 
Evelyn Liddle, Vice President – Performance (New York) 
Team Lead(s): 
Don Wolf, Director – Service Company 
Peter Fitzgerald, Director – Financial Planning & Analysis 
 
Current Status: In Progress 
 
National Grid’s revised New York Gas Management Audit Implementation Plan proposed a 
phased implementation for enhancing the SLAs.  As described in the Implementation Plan, the 
Company implemented FY16 SLAs that include service descriptions, budgets by function (level 4 
budget hierarchy) and updated and enhanced performance metrics.  Providing budget detail at the 
functional level aligns with how management reviews costs and variances and provides a measure 
of consistency between the SLAs and senior leadership business review meetings.  Performance 
metrics include regulatory and business metrics to facilitate the Company’s monitoring and review 
of service delivery.  Failure to meet performance metrics will be taken into consideration when 
determining an employee’s annual performance rating.

Recommendation V-1

reliability work, mandated replacements, growth projects and system planning work identifiable 
over a five-year period. 
• The system plan should include all projects identified based on their relative merit and 
need dates.  It should not be limited by budget amounts. 
• The system plan should be updated annually. 
• It should also include associated project cost estimates, risk scores and resource 
requirements. 
• The integrated system plan should provide input to the Investment Planning process to 
create the Five-Year Investment Plan.

during an integrity assessment. 
• A process for continual evaluation and assessment meeting the requirements of § 
192.937.

• Provisions meeting the requirements of § 192.935 for adding preventive and mitigation 
measures to protect the high consequence area.

Chapter VI – Project Management  
Recommendation VI-2 
Develop an estimating program for gas projects that is consistent with that used for NG USA’s 
electric utilities. 
 
Implementation Leads  
Executive Sponsor: 
Ross Turrini, SVP – Network Strategy 
Team Lead(s): 
Chris Connolly, Director – Project Engineering & Design  
 
Implementation Priority: Medium     
 
 
 
Recommendation Accepted: Yes 
 
Current Status: In Progress 
 
National Grid is developing a Gas Estimating Department (i.e., Estimating Center of Excellence or 
ECoE) within the Gas Systems Engineering Organization.  This new group will support 
development of a consistent estimating end-to-end process including documentation of 
responsibilities and KPI’s to measure performance.  The scope of the Gas ECoE will include 
complex capital projects, as defined by the Gas Project Engineering & Design Playbook.  This 
work includes, but is not limited to, pressure regulating stations, transmission pipeline projects, 
and bridge crossings.  The number of projects this group will manage on an annual basis is 
estimated to be approximately 400 – 450 projects.   
 
The Company is in the process of hiring a Director for the new Gas ECoE, and expects to fill this 
position in September/October 2015.  Thereafter, the Company will staff the new group to 
support the New York Operating Companies and complete the other items identified in Phase I 
(below). 
 
 
Schedule  
National Grid estimates that the Gas ECoE can be established to fulfill the previously identified 
scope of work within 12 to 18 months.

Chapter VIII – Load Forecasting  
Recommendation VIII-4 
Analyze the treatment of energy efficiency goals in the sales, send out and design day 
forecasting processes and models to identify opportunities to improve accuracy and minimize 
impacts of over-forecasting future savings.  In collaboration with PSC staff, determine an 
appropriate approach for handling energy efficiency program goals and achieved savings in 
future modeling. 
 
Implementation Leads  
Executive Sponsor: 
James Cross, VP – Customer Analytics & Risk Management 
Team Lead(s): 
Theodore Poe, Jr., Manager – Gas Forecasting & Analysis 
 
 
Implementation Priority: Low      
 
 
Recommendation Accepted: Yes 
 
Current Status: Pending Review 
Implementation of this recommendation requires the Company to work with internal 
parties and DPS Staff to explore options for handling treatment of energy efficiency 
program results and goals in the forecasting process, including investigating the impact 
of non-achievement of savings on gas procurement decisions.   
In March 2015, the Company and Staff discussed whether the Company should 
consider the full impact of its energy efficiency goals (or some portion of the goals 
based on the Company’s historic success rate) in the peak day gas forecast.  The 
parties acknowledged that, by including the full amount of the efficiency goals, the 
Company could be understating its resulting peak day gas resource requirements.  
Accordingly, it was agreed that the Company will not deduct any impact for its energy 
efficiency goals over and above the success rate in its 2015 Q2 Gas Load Forecasting 
cycle.   
The Company subsequently presented its plan to better address EE goals in the models 
at the annual Winter Supply review. 
 
Schedule  
 
Complete 
Exhibit __ (KSZ-2) 
Page 36 of 45
249

Chapter IX – Supply Procurement  
Recommendation IX-2 
As part of the annual gas supply plan submitted to the PSC, or in a separate filing, specifically

Chapter IX – Supply Procurement  
Recommendation IX-5 
Develop a gas supply performance review process, including a monthly metrics scorecard and 
associated reporting that assesses the overall performance of the Energy Procurement group in 
managing the gas supply portfolio. 
 
Implementation Leads  
Executive Sponsor: 
John Vaughn, VP – Energy Procurement 
Team Lead(s): 
John Allocca, Director – Gas Contracting and Compliance 
Elizabeth Arangio, Director – Gas Supply Planning 
Mark Leippert, Director – Wholesale Gas Supply 
Steve McCauley, Director – Origination and Price Volatility 
Management 
Alex Zhukovsky, Director – Quantitative Risk & Financial 
Reporting 
 
Implementation Priority: Medium     
 
 
 
Recommendation Accepted: Yes 
 
Current Status: In Progress 
 
The Company has developed four performance metrics for the gas supply function:  
 
1. On an annual basis, the Company will forecast Gas Off-System Sales (OSS) Customer 
Savings Targets for Downstate NY and Upstate NY and measure actual performance 
against these targets. 
2. The Company will provide a measure of the volatility of the hedge portfolio relative to the 
overall volatility in the natural gas markets. 
3. The Company will provide a metric of supply cost as a ratio of the actual gas costs to a 
theoretical cost.  The Company will compare the monthly ratio to the 12 month rolling 
average of the ratio.  
4. The Company will provide a metric of the actual weighted average purchase cost compared 
against the weighted average market cost for gas purchases executed for the Downstate NY 
and Upstate NY gas customers. 
 
Schedule 
The Company’s Energy Procurement team met with DPS Staff on September 10, 2015 to present 
its implementation plans for the Audit Report recommendations for Chapter IX.  The Company 
will update its proposal based on feedback from that meeting.

Chapter IX – Supply Procurement  
Recommendation IX-6 
Conduct a thorough investigation of the allocation and assignment of costs, particularly labor 
costs, from Energy Procurement to the NY gas utilities to identify the reasons for the NY Gas 
utilities receiving an apparent disproportionate share of costs.  Verify that costs charged to the 
NY gas utilities by other parts of the Customer functional areas, including the Analytics 
Modeling and Forecasting group that prepares the forecasts used for gas supply planning and 
procurement, and are appropriate and supportable.  Modify cost assignments, time reporting 
and allocation procedures to resolve any identified inconsistencies.  Document the results of the 
investigation and resulting recommendations to the PSC within six months.

Chapter XI – Performance Management 
Recommendation XI-1 
With the FY 2016 planning cycle (beginning in FY15), modify the performance management 
process to replace Elevate 2015 and better align NG-plc, NGUSA, NY jurisdiction, and NY 
Operating Company goals and objectives with a more robust set of performance metrics.  The 
revised performance management program should address/include the following: 
• NG-plc, US, NY and operating company priorities should be aligned but reflect 
individual priorities. 
• All priorities and strategies should have defined performance measures and targets and 
be reported monthly, at a minimum. Ideally metrics would be at the jurisdiction and 
operating company level in addition to the US level. Gaps such as those illustrated in 
Exhibit XI-5 (of the Audit Report) should not exist. 
• Metrics should include leading indicators and should be used to monitor performance 
and address performance issues. 
• The revised SLAs performance measures should be included in the performance 
management system. 
• Operating and process improvement team metrics should continue to be robust and not 
driven to the minimal level of aggregate detail currently represented by Elevate 2015. 
• NY Jurisdictional performance should be routinely reported at an operating company 
level, and should include SLA performance. 
• Any construct developed to communicate the Line of Sight to employees such as Elevate 
2015 should be clearly defined, easy to communicate, tie to the NY/operating company 
objectives/priorities and be supported by metrics that actually measure performance 
against the stated ambition or objective. 
 
Employee performance evaluation objectives and measures should: 
• Be defined and objective. 
• Involve quantified performance targets, wherever possible, and milestones or specific 
deliverables. 
• Reflect US goals and objectives, NY, operating company and business unit priorities, 
along with relevant SLA targets. 
• Consider the employee’s job function and include performance objectives related to that 
job function. 
• Not be artificially tied to broader US ambitions that are not as applicable to the 
employee’s job. 
• Include a manageable set of performance requirements. Too many performance 
requirements make objective performance measurement difficult and can result in a loss 
of focus. 
• Provide objective explanations as to how various performance objectives are weighted or 
used in the rating determination.

Implementation Leads  
Executive Sponsor: 
Ken Daly, President – New York Jurisdiction 
Team Lead(s): 
Evelyn Liddle, Vice President - Performance (New 
York) 
 
Implementation Priority: Medium     
 
 
 
Recommendation Accepted: Yes (with modifications) 
 
Current Status: In Progress 
 
National Grid is committed to ensuring that the US priorities are designed to achieve the 
National Grid plc vision.   The US objectives were established by the jurisdictions based 
on key performance measures.  The objectives represent the specific actions required to 
achieve the performance measures, thereby increasing clarity among employees of their 
role in delivering the objectives.    
 
In addition, consistent with NorthStar’s recommendation, National Grid is strengthening 
its employee performance evaluation P4G processes.  Discussions with senior leadership 
and the materials presented to managers across the organization clarified that employee 
objectives and measures of success must, among other items, (i) reflect US objectives, NY, 
operating company and business unit priorities, along with relevant SLA targets; (ii) 
consider the employee’s job function and include performance objectives related to that job 
function; (iii) not be artificially tied to broader US ambitions that are not as applicable to 
the employee’s job; and (iv) provide objective explanations as to how various performance 
objectives are weighted or used in the rating determination.
